KITAQ SERIES • REFERENCE EDITION

VERIFICATION

Build, execution and display records

From your first line to execution, observation and retesting.
Source snapshot: September 14, 2026 · English edition: September 14, 2026

Verification scope

The records below belong to the September 12 source snapshot. The two compilers and KUROSAKI CLI were built from the collected sources. KOKURA and SARAKURA used local executables fingerprinted in the inventory. These checks do not cover all features, peripherals or physical hardware.

Each entry records compilation, a 120-frame emulator run and its screenshot. Pixel comparisons are separate evidence. Exit code zero alone does not prove correct input, sound or game behavior. Build and runtime evidence is documented in publication checks; test inputs and conditions are recorded there.

API examples: expected images and verification

Each tile program was built and run for 90 frames in KOKURA in both DMG and CGB modes. Verification compares the displayed tile pattern and its blank border against the supplied font, checks the expected foreground colors, and reads the on-screen result. The ROM also reads back the target tile and attribute bytes. A zero exit code alone is not the pass criterion. Writes in these examples occur with the LCD off; hardware behavior and LCD-on transfer timing have not been tested here.

__settile

Write one tile-number byte to the tile map; this selects an existing tile image, not new pixel data.

The destination is always 0x9800 + 32*y + x, regardless of which maps LCDC selects for the background and window.

Expected image: the following tile pattern starts at tile coordinates (x=2, y=3), or pixel coordinates (16,24). Each character occupies one 8×8 tile. Read the pattern from top to bottom and left to right; the surrounding tiles remain blank.

A

On CGB, the sample characters and status text are blue on white. The setup gives palette 0 a blue ink color; it does not change the tile-map attributes. On DMG, the same characters are black on white.

__settileat

Expected image: the following tile pattern starts at tile coordinates (x=2, y=3), or pixel coordinates (16,24). Each character occupies one 8×8 tile. Read the pattern from top to bottom and left to right; the surrounding tiles remain blank.

__settilebg

Write one tile-number byte to the tile map; this selects an existing tile image, not new pixel data.

LCDC bit 3 selects the background map: zero uses 0x9800, one uses 0x9C00. The destination is that base plus 32*y + x.

Expected image: the following tile pattern starts at tile coordinates (x=2, y=3), or pixel coordinates (16,24). Each character occupies one 8×8 tile. Read the pattern from top to bottom and left to right; the surrounding tiles remain blank.

A

On CGB, the sample characters and status text are blue on white. The setup gives palette 0 a blue ink color; it does not change the tile-map attributes. On DMG, the same characters are black on white.

__settilewin

Write one tile-number byte to the tile map; this selects an existing tile image, not new pixel data.

LCDC bit 6 selects the window map: zero uses 0x9800, one uses 0x9C00. These are map coordinates, not the window's screen position.

Expected image: the following tile pattern starts at tile coordinates (x=2, y=3), or pixel coordinates (16,24). Each character occupies one 8×8 tile. Read the pattern from top to bottom and left to right; the surrounding tiles remain blank.

A

On CGB, the sample characters and status text are blue on white. The setup gives palette 0 a blue ink color; it does not change the tile-map attributes. On DMG, the same characters are black on white.

__settileattr

Write only the CGB attribute byte; leave the tile number unchanged. Attribute bits select palette, tile-data bank, flips and priority.

The destination is always 0x9800 + 32*y + x, regardless of which maps LCDC selects for the background and window.

Expected image: the following tile pattern starts at tile coordinates (x=2, y=3), or pixel coordinates (16,24). Each character occupies one 8×8 tile. Read the pattern from top to bottom and left to right; the surrounding tiles remain blank.

A

On CGB the A is blue: attribute value 2 selects the blue palette prepared by this example. On DMG the same character is black because CGB attributes are not applied.

__settileatattr

Write only the CGB attribute byte; leave the tile number unchanged. Attribute bits select palette, tile-data bank, flips and priority.

base explicitly selects the map (0x9800 or 0x9C00); the byte address is base + 32*y + x.

Expected image: the following tile pattern starts at tile coordinates (x=2, y=3), or pixel coordinates (16,24). Each character occupies one 8×8 tile. Read the pattern from top to bottom and left to right; the surrounding tiles remain blank.

A

On CGB the A is blue: attribute value 2 selects the blue palette prepared by this example. On DMG the same character is black because CGB attributes are not applied.

__settilebgattr

Write only the CGB attribute byte; leave the tile number unchanged. Attribute bits select palette, tile-data bank, flips and priority.

LCDC bit 3 selects the background map: zero uses 0x9800, one uses 0x9C00. The destination is that base plus 32*y + x.

Expected image: the following tile pattern starts at tile coordinates (x=2, y=3), or pixel coordinates (16,24). Each character occupies one 8×8 tile. Read the pattern from top to bottom and left to right; the surrounding tiles remain blank.

A

On CGB the A is blue: attribute value 2 selects the blue palette prepared by this example. On DMG the same character is black because CGB attributes are not applied.

__settilecgb

Write a tile number and its CGB attribute. On DMG, only the tile-number write is performed. On CGB, enter with VRAM bank 0 selected; attributes use bank 1 and the call finishes with VBK=0.

The destination is always 0x9800 + 32*y + x, regardless of which maps LCDC selects for the background and window.

Expected image: the following tile pattern starts at tile coordinates (x=2, y=3), or pixel coordinates (16,24). Each character occupies one 8×8 tile. Read the pattern from top to bottom and left to right; the surrounding tiles remain blank.

A

On CGB the A is blue: attribute value 2 selects the blue palette prepared by this example. On DMG the same character is black because CGB attributes are not applied.

__settileatcgb_unsafe

Write a tile number and its CGB attribute. On DMG, only the tile-number write is performed. On CGB, enter with VRAM bank 0 selected; attributes use bank 1 and the call finishes with VBK=0.

base explicitly selects the map (0x9800 or 0x9C00); the byte address is base + 32*y + x.

Expected image: the following tile pattern starts at tile coordinates (x=2, y=3), or pixel coordinates (16,24). Each character occupies one 8×8 tile. Read the pattern from top to bottom and left to right; the surrounding tiles remain blank.

A

On CGB the A is blue: attribute value 2 selects the blue palette prepared by this example. On DMG the same character is black because CGB attributes are not applied.

__settilebgcgb

Write a tile number and its CGB attribute. On DMG, only the tile-number write is performed. On CGB, enter with VRAM bank 0 selected; attributes use bank 1 and the call finishes with VBK=0.

LCDC bit 3 selects the background map: zero uses 0x9800, one uses 0x9C00. The destination is that base plus 32*y + x.

Expected image: the following tile pattern starts at tile coordinates (x=2, y=3), or pixel coordinates (16,24). Each character occupies one 8×8 tile. Read the pattern from top to bottom and left to right; the surrounding tiles remain blank.

A

On CGB the A is blue: attribute value 2 selects the blue palette prepared by this example. On DMG the same character is black because CGB attributes are not applied.

__settilewincgb

Write a tile number and its CGB attribute. On DMG, only the tile-number write is performed. On CGB, enter with VRAM bank 0 selected; attributes use bank 1 and the call finishes with VBK=0.

LCDC bit 6 selects the window map: zero uses 0x9800, one uses 0x9C00. These are map coordinates, not the window's screen position.

Expected image: the following tile pattern starts at tile coordinates (x=2, y=3), or pixel coordinates (16,24). Each character occupies one 8×8 tile. Read the pattern from top to bottom and left to right; the surrounding tiles remain blank.

A

On CGB the A is blue: attribute value 2 selects the blue palette prepared by this example. On DMG the same character is black because CGB attributes are not applied.

__settile_bulk

Copy count consecutive tile-number bytes from src to the VRAM byte address dest. The count is in bytes, not tile-image units; no row-stride adjustment is made.

Waits for writable VRAM when necessary. The LCD-on path uses interrupt masking and can enable interrupts afterward; it is not a promise to preserve the caller's interrupt state or to finish within one VBlank.

Expected image: the following tile pattern starts at tile coordinates (x=2, y=3), or pixel coordinates (16,24). Each character occupies one 8×8 tile. Read the pattern from top to bottom and left to right; the surrounding tiles remain blank.

ABC

On CGB, the sample characters and status text are blue on white. The setup gives palette 0 a blue ink color; it does not change the tile-map attributes. On DMG, the same characters are black on white.

__settileattr_bulk

Waits for writable VRAM when necessary. The LCD-on path uses interrupt masking and can enable interrupts afterward; it is not a promise to preserve the caller's interrupt state or to finish within one VBlank.

Expected image: the following tile pattern starts at tile coordinates (x=2, y=3), or pixel coordinates (16,24). Each character occupies one 8×8 tile. Read the pattern from top to bottom and left to right; the surrounding tiles remain blank.

ABC

On CGB, A, B and C are red, blue and green respectively: attribute bytes 1,2,3 select three different prepared palettes. On DMG all three are black.

__settile_rect

Fill a w by h rectangle in the LCDC-selected BG map with one tile number. Rows are 32 bytes apart; this repeats a tile, rather than copying an image array.

Waits for writable VRAM when necessary. The LCD-on path uses interrupt masking and can enable interrupts afterward; it is not a promise to preserve the caller's interrupt state or to finish within one VBlank.

Expected image: the following tile pattern starts at tile coordinates (x=2, y=3), or pixel coordinates (16,24). Each character occupies one 8×8 tile. Read the pattern from top to bottom and left to right; the surrounding tiles remain blank.

AAA

__settile_row

Copy len successive tile numbers from src horizontally, starting at (x,y) in the LCDC-selected BG map. Source and destination advance by one byte per tile.

Waits for writable VRAM when necessary. The LCD-on path uses interrupt masking and can enable interrupts afterward; it is not a promise to preserve the caller's interrupt state or to finish within one VBlank.

Expected image: the following tile pattern starts at tile coordinates (x=2, y=3), or pixel coordinates (16,24). Each character occupies one 8×8 tile. Read the pattern from top to bottom and left to right; the surrounding tiles remain blank.

ABC

On CGB, the sample characters and status text are blue on white. The setup gives palette 0 a blue ink color; it does not change the tile-map attributes. On DMG, the same characters are black on white.

__settilebg16_buf

Write a 16×16-pixel cell into a RAM tile-map buffer: the four 8×8 tile numbers are tile,tile+1 on top and tile+2,tile+3 below. Values wrap at 256. This does not transfer anything to VRAM.

Expected image: the following tile pattern starts at tile coordinates (x=2, y=2), or pixel coordinates (16,16). Each character occupies one 8×8 tile. Read the pattern from top to bottom and left to right; the surrounding tiles remain blank.

AB
CD

The visible cell contains AB above CD, at tile coordinates (2,2). This is one 16×16-pixel cell at cell coordinates (1,1). Buffer examples first verify that RAM preparation leaves VRAM unchanged, then flush the cell; flush examples prepare the RAM bytes explicitly before transferring them.

On CGB, the sample characters and status text are blue on white. The setup gives palette 0 a blue ink color; it does not change the tile-map attributes. On DMG, the same characters are black on white.

__settilebg16cgb_buf

Write a 16×16-pixel cell into a RAM tile-map buffer: the four 8×8 tile numbers are tile,tile+1 on top and tile+2,tile+3 below. Values wrap at 256. This does not transfer anything to VRAM.

Also write the same attr byte to all four corresponding cells in the separate attribute buffer. Both buffers are ordinary RAM, so this preparation also runs on DMG.

Expected image: the following tile pattern starts at tile coordinates (x=2, y=2), or pixel coordinates (16,16). Each character occupies one 8×8 tile. Read the pattern from top to bottom and left to right; the surrounding tiles remain blank.

AB
CD

The visible cell contains AB above CD, at tile coordinates (2,2). This is one 16×16-pixel cell at cell coordinates (1,1). Buffer examples first verify that RAM preparation leaves VRAM unchanged, then flush the cell; flush examples prepare the RAM bytes explicitly before transferring them.

All four characters are blue on CGB because the four attribute bytes are 2. They are black on DMG.

__settilebg16_flush

Transfer two adjacent tile rows from the RAM buffer to the LCDC-selected BG map. The source offset is 64*y + x; count bytes are copied from that row and from the row 32 bytes below it.

Waits for writable VRAM when necessary. The LCD-on path uses interrupt masking and can enable interrupts afterward; it is not a promise to preserve the caller's interrupt state or to finish within one VBlank.

Expected image: the following tile pattern starts at tile coordinates (x=2, y=2), or pixel coordinates (16,16). Each character occupies one 8×8 tile. Read the pattern from top to bottom and left to right; the surrounding tiles remain blank.

AB
CD

The visible cell contains AB above CD, at tile coordinates (2,2). This is one 16×16-pixel cell at cell coordinates (1,1). Buffer examples first verify that RAM preparation leaves VRAM unchanged, then flush the cell; flush examples prepare the RAM bytes explicitly before transferring them.

On CGB, the sample characters and status text are blue on white. The setup gives palette 0 a blue ink color; it does not change the tile-map attributes. On DMG, the same characters are black on white.

__settilebg16cgb_flush

Transfer two adjacent tile rows from the RAM buffer to the LCDC-selected BG map. The source offset is 64*y + x; count bytes are copied from that row and from the row 32 bytes below it.

Write a tile number and its CGB attribute. On DMG, only the tile-number write is performed. On CGB, enter with VRAM bank 0 selected; attributes use bank 1 and the call finishes with VBK=0.

Expected image: the following tile pattern starts at tile coordinates (x=2, y=2), or pixel coordinates (16,16). Each character occupies one 8×8 tile. Read the pattern from top to bottom and left to right; the surrounding tiles remain blank.

AB
CD

The visible cell contains AB above CD, at tile coordinates (2,2). This is one 16×16-pixel cell at cell coordinates (1,1). Buffer examples first verify that RAM preparation leaves VRAM unchanged, then flush the cell; flush examples prepare the RAM bytes explicitly before transferring them.

All four characters are blue on CGB because the four attribute bytes are 2. They are black on DMG.

entity_init

Initialize the entity pool by resetting every slot.

The complete program teaches the lifetime of a reusable pool slot. Read the labels with the numbers: CREATED 002 after two allocations; AFTER FREE 001 after freeing the first; REUSED ID 000 when the lowest free ID is reused; AFTER CLEAR 000 after clearing the pool. It checks null lookup after release and reset fields after reuse. FAILED CHECKS 000 means these checks matched the expected values.

The GB traversal calls each callback for the two active entities: UPDATE CALLS 002 and DRAW CALLS 002. The sample's update callback moves both one tile to the right. Its drawing callback places A at tile (3,12) and B at (7,12), representing the two objects. These are background font tiles drawn by the callback, not automatically allocated sprites.

entity_update_all

Visit active slots in ascending ID order and call fn(id) once for each. The callback implements movement, collisions or timers; the pool does not supply that logic. A null callback does nothing.

Count the occupied entity slots without modifying them.

The complete program teaches the lifetime of a reusable pool slot. Read the labels with the numbers: CREATED 002 after two allocations; AFTER FREE 001 after freeing the first; REUSED ID 000 when the lowest free ID is reused; AFTER CLEAR 000 after clearing the pool. It checks null lookup after release and reset fields after reuse. FAILED CHECKS 000 means these checks matched the expected values.

The FC traversal calls each callback for the two active entities: UPDATE CALLS 002 and DRAW CALLS 002. The sample's update callback moves both one tile to the right. Its drawing callback places A at tile (3,12) and B at (7,12), representing the two objects. These are background font tiles drawn by the callback, not automatically allocated sprites.

input_init

Clear the current, previous, pressed, released and repeat masks, and reset all eight repeat timers to INPUT_REPEAT_DELAY. This initializes the input library without reading the controller. A button held during the first subsequent update is detected as a new press.

This program demonstrates the difference between held state, a new press, release and automatic repeat. Hold A for 40 updates, then release it. FIRST DOWN, FIRST PRESS and FIRST REPEAT are 1; FIRST CURRENT is 16. HELD TICKS is 40, PRESS COUNT is 1 and REPEAT COUNT is 5. On release, RELEASE is 1, LAST CURRENT is 0 and LAST PREVIOUS is 16. FAILED CHECKS must be 0. Holding A for a different duration changes the counts; the program checks the repeat timing against the duration it actually observed.

Complete example program · Recorded checks

__readpad

Read directions and action buttons from JOYP and combine them into the shared 8-bit button mask. Selects each JOYP group, performs two reads for settling, then restores the group-selection bits to 0x30.

Use this program to compare the three button-mask layouts and the packed press result. Hold A and Right together. ALL KEYS is 17; DIRECTIONS and BUTTONS LOW are each 1. With a previous mask of 0, EX CURRENT and EX NEW PRESS are both 17. Passing the current mask to the next read makes HELD PRESS 0. FAILED CHECKS must be 0. The values remain on screen after the snapshot, so releasing the buttons does not erase them.

Build log

Build log

Complete example program · Recorded checks

__readpaddir

Read only the JOYP direction group. The result uses bits 0–3: Right=1, Left=2, Up=4, Down=8; bits 4–7 are zero. Action buttons are ignored and JOYP selection is restored to 0x30.

Use this program to compare the three button-mask layouts and the packed press result. Hold A and Right together. ALL KEYS is 17; DIRECTIONS and BUTTONS LOW are each 1. With a previous mask of 0, EX CURRENT and EX NEW PRESS are both 17. Passing the current mask to the next read makes HELD PRESS 0. FAILED CHECKS must be 0. The values remain on screen after the snapshot, so releasing the buttons does not erase them.

Complete example program · Recorded checks

__readpadbtn

Read only the JOYP action-button group as a low nibble: A=1, B=2, Select=4, Start=8. These values are not BTN_A through BTN_START; those shared masks occupy the high nibble. JOYP selection is restored to 0x30.

Use this program to compare the three button-mask layouts and the packed press result. Hold A and Right together. ALL KEYS is 17; DIRECTIONS and BUTTONS LOW are each 1. With a previous mask of 0, EX CURRENT and EX NEW PRESS are both 17. Passing the current mask to the next read makes HELD PRESS 0. FAILED CHECKS must be 0. The values remain on screen after the snapshot, so releasing the buttons does not erase them.

Complete example program · Recorded checks

__readpadex

Read all buttons and compare them with the supplied previous held mask. Pack the current mask into the low byte and newly pressed bits, current & ~previous, into the high byte. No previous state is stored internally and no release or repeat mask is returned.

Use this program to compare the three button-mask layouts and the packed press result. Hold A and Right together. ALL KEYS is 17; DIRECTIONS and BUTTONS LOW are each 1. With a previous mask of 0, EX CURRENT and EX NEW PRESS are both 17. Passing the current mask to the next read makes HELD PRESS 0. FAILED CHECKS must be 0. The values remain on screen after the snapshot, so releasing the buttons does not erase them.

__pad_read1

Latch both controllers with a 1-to-0 write to $4016, then read the eight D0 serial bits from controller 1 at $4016. Return one NES-format held-button snapshot without retrying or calculating edges.

This program compares both controller ports, ordinary and repeated reads, mask filters and the shorthand macros. Hold A + Right on controller 1 and B + Left on controller 2. RAW P1, SAFE P1 and ALIAS P1 are 129; the three P2 values are 66. BUTTONS P1 is 1 and DIRS P1 is 128: filtering directions preserves their high-bit positions. FAILED CHECKS must be 0. The captured values remain on screen.

__pad_dirs

Complete example program · Recorded checks

nes_pad_poll

Strobe $4016, read controller 1's eight serial D0 bits, and update nes_pad1_prev, nes_pad1_cur, nes_pad1_pressed and nes_pad1_released. This is a single raw read, without the matching-read retry used by safe intrinsics. A final read of $4017 does not populate controller-2 state.

This program teaches the poll → repeat-step → query order and shows that the NES helpers return masked bits, not Boolean 1. With nes_pad_repeat_config(3, 1), hold B for 40 updates, then release it. FIRST HELD, FIRST TRIGGER and FIRST REPEAT are 2. HELD TICKS is 40, PULSE COUNT is 19 and RELEASE BITS is 2. The first held repeat follows four updates after the initial pulse; later pulses are two updates apart. FAILED CHECKS must be 0. A different hold duration changes the pulse count.

Build log

Complete example program · Recorded checks

__padrep_init

Initialize a caller-owned five-byte repeat state: clear the active action and both counters, and store the supplied initial delay and repeat interval. No buttons are read and no repeat pulse is emitted.

This program feeds controlled button snapshots into four independent states to show exactly when a repeat fires. With delay 3 and interval 2, eight calls numbered 0–7 emit pulses on calls 0, 3, 5 and 7. RIGHT PULSES, DOWN PULSES, ACTION PULSES and ALIAS PULSES therefore each display 004. The general schedulers receive A and B together and select A (16). The program also checks initialization, reset, two-direction priority, release, adoption of an already held direction, zero timing values and an expression argument. CHECKS displays 057 and FAILED CHECKS must display 000. This is a deterministic state-processing example; it does not wait for you to press buttons.

Build log

Complete example program · Recorded checks

__padrep_reset

Cancel the active action and clear both elapsed counters while preserving the configured delay and interval. Use this when closing a menu or changing input focus so a previous hold does not carry its repeat timing into the next interaction.

__padrep_lr

Advance a single Left/Right repeat action. A new Right press emits 1 immediately; otherwise a new Left press emits 2. If both are newly pressed, Right wins. Without a new press, holding both directions returns 0 and freezes the counters; holding neither clears the runtime state.

This program feeds controlled button snapshots into four independent states to show exactly when a repeat fires. __padrep_down

Advance a repeat action for Down only. A new Down press returns 8 immediately and restarts the counters. Held Down produces later pulses according to the state's timing; releasing Down clears the runtime state. Other buttons do not trigger this scheduler.

Complete example program · Recorded checks

__padrep

Schedule one repeating action selected by mask. Among newly pressed eligible bits, choose the lowest set bit and emit it immediately. Otherwise keep the active action while it remains eligible and held. If that action ends, adopt the lowest remaining held bit without an immediate pulse. No eligible held bits clears the runtime state.

This program feeds controlled button snapshots into four independent states to show exactly when a repeat fires. With delay 3 and interval 2, eight calls numbered 0–7 emit pulses on calls 0, 3, 5 and 7. RIGHT PULSES, DOWN PULSES, ACTION PULSES and ALIAS PULSES therefore each display 004. The general schedulers receive A and B together and select A (16). The program also checks initialization, reset, two-direction priority, release, adoption of an already held direction, zero timing values and an expression argument. CHECKS displays 057 and FAILED CHECKS must display 000. This is a deterministic state-processing example; it does not wait for you to press buttons.

Build log

Complete example program · Recorded checks

__padrep_mask

__padrep_mask is an alternative spelling of __padrep; both enter the same compiler implementation. It has the same single-action selection, timing, state layout and return values. The explicit name can make a masked action scheduler easier to recognize at a call site.

Schedule one repeating action selected by mask. Among newly pressed eligible bits, choose the lowest set bit and emit it immediately. Otherwise keep the active action while it remains eligible and held. If that action ends, adopt the lowest remaining held bit without an immediate pulse. No eligible held bits clears the runtime state.

This program feeds controlled button snapshots into four independent states to show exactly when a repeat fires. With delay 3 and interval 2, eight calls numbered 0–7 emit pulses on calls 0, 3, 5 and 7. RIGHT PULSES, DOWN PULSES, ACTION PULSES and ALIAS PULSES therefore each display 004. The general schedulers receive A and B together and select A (16). The program also checks initialization, reset, two-direction priority, release, adoption of an already held direction, zero timing values and an expression argument. CHECKS displays 057 and FAILED CHECKS must display 000. This is a deterministic state-processing example; it does not wait for you to press buttons.

Build log

Build log

Complete example program · Recorded checks

__pad_read1_d1

Read eight serial bits from expansion input D1 at $4016. Write 1 then 0 to $4016 to latch the controller streams, sample bit 1 eight times, and place successive samples into result bits 0–7. This is one read of the D1 channel for port 1; it does not read the ordinary D0 controller channel or retry for matching samples.

This program contrasts ordinary D0 input with expansion D1 and the microphone's digital level. Hold A on controller 1 and B on controller 2 until the snapshot is captured. NORMAL P1 and NORMAL P2 display 001 and 002. In the supplied KUROSAKI run, D1 PORT 1, D1 PORT 2, EXP ALIAS 1, EXP ALIAS 2, MIC LEVEL and MIC DIRECT all display 000 because those signal lines are not emulated. The displayed values then stay fixed. With suitable peripherals and an implementation of their signal lines, the expansion and microphone values depend on the actual inputs; consult the bit layouts above rather than expecting these zeros.

The published KUROSAKI controller model returns only D0 plus fixed bit 6. D1 and the microphone bit are therefore always 0 in that emulator, even while ordinary controller buttons are held. The supplied run verifies compilation, ordinary-button input and this zero-signal observation. Nonzero peripheral signals, electrical timing and real-device compatibility remain unverified; a zero here is not proof that an attached device works or that these functions cannot read it on hardware.

Complete example program · Recorded checks

__exp_pad_read1

__exp_pad_read1 calls exactly the same compiler helper as __pad_read1_d1. It reads the expansion D1 signal at $4016 with the same latch sequence, eight-bit result and side effects. This name identifies the expansion-input purpose; it does not enable another device mode or combine D0 and D1.

__joypad2p_voice

__joypad2p_voice is another entry name for the __mic_read2p helper. Both read the same digital microphone bit. The word “voice” does not imply audio processing; separate calls take separate samples and can differ if the signal changes between them.

Read the digital signal for the second Famicom controller's microphone from $4016 bit 2. The helper reads the register once, masks 0x04 and normalizes the result to 0 or 1 without inversion. It does not latch controllers, record audio, measure volume or recognize speech.

This program contrasts ordinary D0 input with expansion D1 and the microphone's digital level. Hold A on controller 1 and B on controller 2 until the snapshot is captured. Complete example program · Recorded checks

vram_init

Initialize the deferred VRAM command queue to empty and clear its overflow latch. This does not erase the screen, upload tile graphics or allocate hardware VRAM.

This complete program demonstrates deferred writes, queue accounting and overflow recovery. Seven drawing calls use 7 of 32 command slots on GB, or 52 of 128 command bytes on FC: CAPACITY/USED/FREE reads 032/007/025 or 128/052/076. AFTER FLUSH and FAILED CHECKS must be 000; OVERFLOW SEEN must be 001. An intentional full-queue rejection tests the latch. Reading or flushing does not clear it; clearing the queue does. On FC, an uncommitted direct flush must leave the pending queue intact.

Image coordinates are pixels from the upper-left corner. Expect solid 8 × 8 squares at (16,16) and (32,16), a solid 32 × 24 rectangle at (16,32), and a 40 × 8 horizontal bar at (80,64). The final VBlank write adds an 8 × 8 square at (144,80). The discarded write at (144,24) must leave that cell blank. DMG uses black shapes on white, CGB uses red, blue and green shapes on white, and FC uses red, blue and green shapes on black. These emulator images do not constitute real-hardware verification.

Three original 8 × 8 patterns distinguish the copy operations: tile 1 is solid; tile 2 fills its left four columns; tile 3 is a left-aligned triangle widening from one pixel at the top to eight at the bottom. At (80,32), the 3 × 2 tile block must read [1,2,3] / [3,0,2], where 0 is blank. Its first source byte changes after queuing, so a solid tile there proves that the source pointer is retained. At (16,64), the copy shows [1,2,3]; at (48,64), the tiles alias shows [2,3]. Their dimensions are 24 × 16, 24 × 8 and 16 × 8 pixels respectively.

In CGB mode, the two upper squares, the three-pattern copy and the final VBlank square are red. The 32 × 24 rectangle and two-pattern copy are blue. The packed 3 × 2 block and 40 × 8 bar are green. Text remains black on white. Color checks cover each drawn pixel as well as geometry.

On FC, an uncommitted direct flush must leave the pending queue intact.

Image coordinates are pixels from the upper-left corner. Expect solid 8 × 8 squares at (16,16) and (32,16), a solid 32 × 24 rectangle at (16,32), and a 40 × 8 horizontal bar at (80,64). The final VBlank write adds an 8 × 8 square at (144,80). The discarded write at (144,24) must leave that cell blank. DMG uses black shapes on white, CGB uses red, blue and green shapes on white, and FC uses red, blue and green shapes on black. These emulator images do not constitute real-hardware verification.

Three original 8 × 8 patterns distinguish the copy operations: tile 1 is solid; tile 2 fills its left four columns; tile 3 is a left-aligned triangle widening from one pixel at the top to eight at the bottom. At (80,32), the 3 × 2 tile block must read [1,2,3] / [3,0,2], where 0 is blank. Its first source byte changes after queuing, so a solid tile there proves that the source pointer is retained. At (16,64), the copy shows [1,2,3]; at (48,64), the tiles alias shows [2,3]. Their dimensions are 24 × 16, 24 × 8 and 16 × 8 pixels respectively.

In CGB mode, the two upper squares, the three-pattern copy and the final VBlank square are red. The 32 × 24 rectangle and two-pattern copy are blue. The packed 3 × 2 block and 40 × 8 bar are green. Text remains black on white. Color checks cover each drawn pixel as well as geometry.

Build log

vram_get_queue_capacity

Return the total capacity of the command buffer, regardless of how much is currently used.

This complete program demonstrates deferred writes, queue accounting and overflow recovery. Seven drawing calls use 7 of 32 command slots on GB, or 52 of 128 command bytes on FC: CAPACITY/USED/FREE reads 032/007/025 or 128/052/076. AFTER FLUSH and FAILED CHECKS must be 000; OVERFLOW SEEN must be 001. An intentional full-queue rejection tests the latch. Reading or flushing does not clear it; clearing the queue does. On FC, an uncommitted direct flush must leave the pending queue intact.

vram_flush_now

Execute every queued command in insertion order, then set the used count to 0. No VBlank wait is performed here. The called safe VRAM intrinsics handle writable periods when the LCD is on, but a large queue can span several scanlines or frames. The overflow latch is preserved.

This complete program demonstrates deferred writes, queue accounting and overflow recovery. Seven drawing calls use 7 of 32 command slots on GB, or 52 of 128 command bytes on FC: CAPACITY/USED/FREE reads 032/007/025 or 128/052/076. vram_flush

Wait with __wait_vblank, then call vram_flush_now. Use this for a modest batch of queued screen changes. The single wait does not impose a transfer budget or guarantee that the entire batch finishes within one VBlank.

vram_queue_memcpy

Queue a copy of len consecutive bytes from src to dst. Source and destination advance by one byte, without a tile-row stride. This can transfer map bytes or writable tile-pattern bytes; the function does not convert formats or infer how many bytes a tile occupies.

vram_get_queue_free

Return total command-buffer capacity minus current usage. Check whether the entire next record or batch fits before appending it; this getter does not reserve space.

This complete program demonstrates deferred writes, queue accounting and overflow recovery. Seven drawing calls use 7 of 32 command slots on GB, or 52 of 128 command bytes on FC: CAPACITY/USED/FREE reads 032/007/025 or 128/052/076. AFTER FLUSH and FAILED CHECKS must be 000; OVERFLOW SEEN must be 001. An intentional full-queue rejection tests the latch. Reading or flushing does not clear it; clearing the queue does. On FC, an uncommitted direct flush must leave the pending queue intact.

Image coordinates are pixels from the upper-left corner. Expect solid 8 × 8 squares at (16,16) and (32,16), a solid 32 × 24 rectangle at (16,32), and a 40 × 8 horizontal bar at (80,64). The final VBlank write adds an 8 × 8 square at (144,80). The discarded write at (144,24) must leave that cell blank. DMG uses black shapes on white, CGB uses red, blue and green shapes on white, and FC uses red, blue and green shapes on black. These emulator images do not constitute real-hardware verification.

Three original 8 × 8 patterns distinguish the copy operations: tile 1 is solid; tile 2 fills its left four columns; tile 3 is a left-aligned triangle widening from one pixel at the top to eight at the bottom. At (80,32), the 3 × 2 tile block must read [1,2,3] / [3,0,2], where 0 is blank. Its first source byte changes after queuing, so a solid tile there proves that the source pointer is retained. At (16,64), the copy shows [1,2,3]; at (48,64), the tiles alias shows [2,3]. Their dimensions are 24 × 16, 24 × 8 and 16 × 8 pixels respectively.

On FC, red identifies the two upper squares, the three-pattern copy and the final VBlank square. Blue identifies the 32 × 24 rectangle and the two-pattern copy. Green identifies the 3 × 2 tile block and the 40 × 8 bar. The background is black; each drawn pixel is checked for both position and color.

vram_flush_now

Call __vramq_exec immediately. It executes only when the ready flag has been set by __vramq_commit; an uncommitted queue remains untouched. A completed execution clears the length and ready flag, but preserves overflow. This function neither commits the queue nor waits for safe PPU timing.

vram_flush

Commit the intrinsic queue with __vramq_commit, then wait with __nmi_wait for the NMI path. The default handler consumes a ready queue. Keep NMI enabled and its handler functional; this call does not split a large transfer across successive VBlanks.

Complete example program · Recorded checks

__vram_memcpy

Copy len bytes from src to dst immediately, in increasing address order. The function returns after the copy finishes; it does not enqueue work. Source and destination advance by one byte, without tilemap row strides or tile-format conversion.

This example uses __vram_memcpy to place three map bytes at tile (2,2), pixel (16,16). Expect the three patterns [1,2,3] from left to right in a 24 × 8 area. Neighboring cells must remain blank. The full program places each tested copy or fill on its own row.

The supplied original graphics use tile 1 for a solid 8 × 8 square, tile 2 for its left four columns, and tile 3 for a left-aligned triangle that widens by one pixel per row from 1 to 8 pixels. DMG uses black on white; CGB distinguishes the operations with colors while keeping text black.

The square at pixel (16,88) must remain after zero-length safe and unsafe calls. With the LCD on, safe copy and fill add squares at (16,96) and (32,96). FAILED CHECKS must read 000. The emulator tests compare the drawn pixels as well as the counter. CGB uses bank 0 in this example; other banks and physical hardware are not tested here.

In CGB mode, the operation rows from MEMCPY through FILL UNSAFE cycle through red, blue and green. The ZERO COUNT square is red. In LCD ON, the left square is blue and the right square is green. Text stays black. The checks verify these colors at every drawn pixel.

__vram_memset

Immediately fill len consecutive bytes starting at dst with the byte value. This repeats one byte, not a whole tile pattern. A map-byte fill repeats a tile number; a pattern-byte fill instead changes the encoded pixel planes.

This example uses __vram_memset to fill three map bytes at tile (2,7), pixel (16,56), with tile number 1. Expect a solid 24 × 8 bar with blank neighboring cells. The count is computed from variables or a function result; all three bytes must still receive value 1.

The supplied original graphics use tile 1 for a solid 8 × 8 square, tile 2 for its left four columns, and tile 3 for a left-aligned triangle that widens by one pixel per row from 1 to 8 pixels. DMG uses black on white; CGB distinguishes the operations with colors while keeping text black.

The square at pixel (16,88) must remain after zero-length safe and unsafe calls. With the LCD on, safe copy and fill add squares at (16,96) and (32,96). FAILED CHECKS must read 000. The emulator tests compare the drawn pixels as well as the counter. CGB uses bank 0 in this example; other banks and physical hardware are not tested here.

In CGB mode, the operation rows from MEMCPY through FILL UNSAFE cycle through red, blue and green. The ZERO COUNT square is red. In LCD ON, the left square is blue and the right square is green. Text stays black. The checks verify these colors at every drawn pixel.

Complete example program · Recorded checks

__vramq_clear

Discard all pending intrinsic-queue records and cancel readiness by setting both length and ready to 0. It does not write VRAM or clear the overflow latch. To reset that latch, call __vramq_clear_overflow separately.

The complete program teaches queue capacity, retained pointers, independent reset flags and immediate versus NMI execution. Expect CAPACITY 128, ENCODED BYTES 030, AFTER EXEC 000, OVERFLOW SEEN 001 and FAILED CHECKS 000. Six records use 30 buffer bytes but perform 512 byte writes. OVERFLOW SEEN preserves the earlier intentional rejection; it is not a claim that the final latch remains set.

The blue copy starts at pixel (0,16), and the green fill at (0,88). Each covers seven full 256-pixel rows of tiles, then 248 pixels of the eighth tile row: 255 tiles total. The final 8 × 8 cell stays black. The copy repeats solid, left-half and triangular 8 × 8 patterns; its first tile is a triangle because the source changed after enqueueing. The fill stays solid even after its caller variable changes. Both shape and color are checked.

At y=160, expect a red solid square at x=16, a red left-half tile at x=224 and a red solid square at x=240. The first survives zero-length copy/fill records; the middle proves default-NMI consumption; the last is a single put. The cancelled cell at (248,160) must remain black. The long transfers run with rendering and NMI off; only the final small write uses NMI. This is NROM emulator evidence, not a real-hardware test.

Build log

Complete example program · Recorded checks

__vramq_put

Append one PPU address and byte value as a four-byte record. The value is copied into the queue now, and written when the queue executes. A single nametable tile update is one use; the address may instead name another writable PPU byte.

__vramq_copy

Append one six-byte record that copies len bytes from src to ppu_addr at execution time. The queue retains the source pointer, not the payload. __vramq_commit

Set the queue's ready flag to 1 so the executor may consume it. This does not copy payloads, execute PPU writes or wait for NMI. Once committed, keep the queue and retained sources stable until execution finishes. With NMI enabled, the default handler can consume it asynchronously.

The complete program teaches queue capacity, retained pointers, independent reset flags and immediate versus NMI execution. Expect CAPACITY 128, ENCODED BYTES 030, AFTER EXEC 000, OVERFLOW SEEN 001 and FAILED CHECKS 000. Six records use 30 buffer bytes but perform 512 byte writes. OVERFLOW SEEN preserves the earlier intentional rejection; it is not a claim that the final latch remains set.

The blue copy starts at pixel (0,16), and the green fill at (0,88). Each covers seven full 256-pixel rows of tiles, then 248 pixels of the eighth tile row: 255 tiles total. The final 8 × 8 cell stays black. The copy repeats solid, left-half and triangular 8 × 8 patterns; its first tile is a triangle because the source changed after enqueueing. The fill stays solid even after its caller variable changes. Both shape and color are checked.

At y=160, expect a red solid square at x=16, a red left-half tile at x=224 and a red solid square at x=240. The first survives zero-length copy/fill records; the middle proves default-NMI consumption; the last is a single put. The cancelled cell at (248,160) must remain black. The long transfers run with rendering and NMI off; only the final small write uses NMI. This is NROM emulator evidence, not a real-hardware test.

Complete example program · Recorded checks

__vramq_exec

Execute a committed queue immediately, in record order. Without readiness, return without changing the queue. After execution, clear length and readiness but preserve overflow. This routine does not wait for safe PPU timing, impose a transfer budget or restore scroll; arrange these conditions at the call site.

The complete program teaches queue capacity, retained pointers, independent reset flags and immediate versus NMI execution. Expect CAPACITY 128, ENCODED BYTES 030, AFTER EXEC 000, OVERFLOW SEEN 001 and FAILED CHECKS 000. Six records use 30 buffer bytes but perform 512 byte writes. OVERFLOW SEEN preserves the earlier intentional rejection; it is not a claim that the final latch remains set.

The blue copy starts at pixel (0,16), and the green fill at (0,88). Each covers seven full 256-pixel rows of tiles, then 248 pixels of the eighth tile row: 255 tiles total. The final 8 × 8 cell stays black. The copy repeats solid, left-half and triangular 8 × 8 patterns; its first tile is a triangle because the source changed after enqueueing. The fill stays solid even after its caller variable changes. Both shape and color are checked.

At y=160, expect a red solid square at x=16, a red left-half tile at x=224 and a red solid square at x=240. The first survives zero-length copy/fill records; the middle proves default-NMI consumption; the last is a single put. The cancelled cell at (248,160) must remain black. The long transfers run with rendering and NMI off; only the final small write uses NMI. This is NROM emulator evidence, not a real-hardware test.

nes_vram_put

Macro from nes_game.h: expands to the intrinsic shown in the syntax. Include this header; no C implementation file is needed for this operation.

Append one PPU address and byte value as a four-byte record. The value is copied into the queue now, and written when the queue executes. A single nametable tile update is one use; the address may instead name another writable PPU byte.

The complete program uses nes_game.h macros for put, copy, fill, commit and queue clearing. It also uses __vramq_* inspection and execution intrinsics to check their effects. The title is VRAM QUEUE MACROS. The large transfers execute with rendering disabled; committing a large transfer does not make it fit within VBlank.

nes_vram_copy

Macro from nes_game.h: expands to the intrinsic shown in the syntax. Include this header; no C implementation file is needed for this operation.

Append one six-byte record that copies len bytes from src to ppu_addr at execution time. The queue retains the source pointer, not the payload. nes_vram_commit

Macro from nes_game.h: expands to the intrinsic shown in the syntax. Include this header; no C implementation file is needed for this operation.

Set the queue's ready flag to 1 so the executor may consume it. This does not copy payloads, execute PPU writes or wait for NMI. Once committed, keep the queue and retained sources stable until execution finishes. With NMI enabled, the default handler can consume it asynchronously.

The complete program uses nes_game.h macros for put, copy, fill, commit and queue clearing. It also uses __vramq_* inspection and execution intrinsics to check their effects. Complete example program · Recorded checks

nes_vram_queue_clear

Discard every pending C runtime queue record and reset both nes_vram_queue_used and nes_vram_queue_overflow to zero. No PPU writes occur; stored payload bytes are not erased. The compiler’s intrinsic queue is unaffected.

The sample first fills all 192 queue bytes and deliberately rejects another record. It clears that batch, rejects length 128, then queues five records using 145 bytes for 255 PPU writes. Expect CAPACITY 192, ENCODED BYTES 145, AFTER FLUSH 000, REJECTED LENGTH 001, OVERFLOW SEEN 001 and FAILED CHECKS 000. The rejection counters preserve observations made earlier in this program; they do not describe the final latch state.

Blue copied tiles begin at (0,16); green filled tiles begin at (0,64). Each area contains 127 tiles: three full rows and 31 tiles in the fourth, with the last 8×8 cell black. The blue sequence repeats solid, half-width and triangular tiles; its first tile stays solid even after the source changes. At y=112, the red square at x=16 survives zero-length records, the red half-tile at x=224 proves automatic NMI flushing, and the cancelled cell at x=248 stays black. Verification compares 18,432 pixels and checks zero VRAM writes during active rendering. Physical hardware is untested.

nes_vram_queue_try_write

Append a literal-data record to the C runtime queue. Copy len source bytes into the queue during this call, then publish the new used length. The PPU transfer happens when the queue is flushed. Changing or reusing the source after a successful call does not change the queued data.

nes_vram_queue_nmi_flush

Execute all pending C runtime queue records in order, without a commit flag. Seek the PPU address for each record and copy its stored payload or repeat its fill byte. On completion, set nes_vram_queue_used to zero while preserving the overflow latch. An empty queue causes no PPU access.

The sample first fills all 192 queue bytes and deliberately rejects another record. It clears that batch, rejects length 128, then queues five records using 145 bytes for 255 PPU writes. Recorded checks (13)

Complete example program · Recorded checks

nes_wait_nmi

Wait until nes_nmi_counter differs from the value captured on entry. The eight-bit counter is advanced by runtime.c's __nes_nmi after flushing its pending queue; wrap from 255 to 0 is handled by the inequality comparison. Enable NMI and use that handler, or provide an equivalent counter update. This polling loop has no timeout and does not enable NMI, restore scrolling or enforce an exact elapsed frame duration.

The runtime example teaches direct transfer and the two wait methods. Expect a blue pattern of solid tiles, left-half bars and triangles at (0,16), and a solid green block at (0,96); each block is 256×64 pixels, from 256 tile bytes. The first blue tile remains solid after changing the source. At y=208, red markers at x=16, 224 and 240 show seek, zero-length write and zero-length fill; they are a solid 8×8 square, a 4×8 left-half bar and an eight-row triangle. Both wait indicators read 1 and FAILED CHECKS reads 0.

Recorded checks (14)

Complete example program · Recorded checks

nes_vblank_wait

Poll PPUSTATUS bit 7 for a fresh VBlank indication, first consuming any already-set indication. This works with NMI disabled and does not call a queue consumer or change the runtime NMI counter. Every status read clears the VBlank flag and resets the shared scroll/address write latch. Use this polling method separately from NMI-driven scheduling: status reads can affect NMI timing. There is no timeout, transfer or scroll restoration.

The runtime example teaches direct transfer and the two wait methods. Expect a blue pattern of solid tiles, left-half bars and triangles at (0,16), and a solid green block at (0,96); each block is 256×64 pixels, from 256 tile bytes. The first blue tile remains solid after changing the source. At y=208, red markers at x=16, 224 and 240 show seek, zero-length write and zero-length fill; they are a solid 8×8 square, a 4×8 left-half bar and an eight-row triangle. Both wait indicators read 1 and FAILED CHECKS reads 0.

Recorded checks (14)

Complete example program · Recorded checks

nes_ppu_seek

Set the PPU address for subsequent PPUDATA access. runtime.c provides nes_ppu_seek(ppu_addr): it reads PPUSTATUS to reset the shared write latch, then writes the high and low address bytes. ppu.c provides a separate nes_ppu_seek(hi, lo) that does not reset the latch. These are different functions with the same name; select one implementation and never link both.

The runtime example teaches direct transfer and the two wait methods. Expect a blue pattern of solid tiles, left-half bars and triangles at (0,16), and a solid green block at (0,96); each block is 256×64 pixels, from 256 tile bytes. The first blue tile remains solid after changing the source. At y=208, red markers at x=16, 224 and 240 show seek, zero-length write and zero-length fill; they are a solid 8×8 square, a 4×8 left-half bar and an eight-row triangle. Both wait indicators read 1 and FAILED CHECKS reads 0.

FAILED CHECKS reads 0; this program does not test the runtime wait functions.

Build log

Recorded checks (14)

Complete example program · Recorded checks

cgb_bg_color

Write one packed RGB15 color to the background/window palette store immediately. The palette and color indices select one of 32 color slots; this does not change tile data or select a tile's palette.

This program teaches how palette contents affect the same tile pattern differently in BG and OBJ. Each triangle is 8 × 8 pixels; its top row has one pixel on the left, expanding to a full bottom row. At x = 16, 32, 48, 64, 80, 96, 112, the BG row at y = 24 is red, blue, green, red, blue, green, red. The OBJ row at y = 64 is blue, green, red, blue, green, red, blue. The columns use single-color, component, indexed-range, full-palette, absolute-range, canonical-register and alias-register writes, respectively.

At y = 88, the seven 8 × 8 cells show: red triangle (x = 16), blue one-pixel square outline from bank 1 (32), horizontally reflected green triangle (48), vertically reflected red triangle (64), blue triangle reflected both ways (80), green triangle over a red sprite with red visible through color-zero pixels (96), and a full red sprite in front of the BG triangle as a priority control (112). Coordinates are screen pixels measured from the upper-left corner. Blank space and black text remain on white.

FAILED CHECKS 000 confirms RGB masking, truth-valued flags (including input 2), palette index wrapping, writes crossing a palette boundary, clamping at slot 31, null/zero-length no-ops and palette readback while LCD is off. Four target/hardware combinations are tested: dual-mode ROM on DMG and CGB, DMG-only on DMG, and CGB-only on CGB. On DMG, CGB MODE is 0, all triangles are black, the bank/flip attributes have no display effect, and the last two bottom cells are black squares. CGB MODE is 1 in CGB tests. Real hardware and LCD-on palette contention are not covered.

Complete example program · Recorded checks

cgb_bg_rgb

Pack the red, green and blue components with cgb_rgb15, then write one color to the background/window palette store. Use this when components are available separately instead of a packed word.

This program teaches how palette contents affect the same tile pattern differently in BG and OBJ. Each triangle is 8 × 8 pixels; its top row has one pixel on the left, expanding to a full bottom row. At x = 16, 32, 48, 64, 80, 96, 112, the BG row at y = 24 is red, blue, green, red, blue, green, red. The OBJ row at y = 64 is blue, green, red, blue, green, red, blue. The columns use single-color, component, indexed-range, full-palette, absolute-range, canonical-register and alias-register writes, respectively.

At y = 88, the seven 8 × 8 cells show: red triangle (x = 16), blue one-pixel square outline from bank 1 (32), horizontally reflected green triangle (48), vertically reflected red triangle (64), blue triangle reflected both ways (80), green triangle over a red sprite with red visible through color-zero pixels (96), and a full red sprite in front of the BG triangle as a priority control (112). Coordinates are screen pixels measured from the upper-left corner. Blank space and black text remain on white.

FAILED CHECKS 000 confirms RGB masking, truth-valued flags (including input 2), palette index wrapping, writes crossing a palette boundary, clamping at slot 31, null/zero-length no-ops and palette readback while LCD is off. Four target/hardware combinations are tested: dual-mode ROM on DMG and CGB, DMG-only on DMG, and CGB-only on CGB. On DMG, CGB MODE is 0, all triangles are black, the bank/flip attributes have no display effect, and the last two bottom cells are black squares. CGB MODE is 1 in CGB tests. Real hardware and LCD-on palette contention are not covered.

cgb_bg_colors_raw

__cgb_safe_set_bcps

Write the background/window palette byte-index register BCPS/BGPI at $FF68 on CGB. __cgb_safe_set_bgpi writes the same register. This selects where a subsequent data-register access goes; it does not itself write a color.

This program teaches how palette contents affect the same tile pattern differently in BG and OBJ. Each triangle is 8 × 8 pixels; its top row has one pixel on the left, expanding to a full bottom row. Coordinates are screen pixels measured from the upper-left corner. Blank space and black text remain on white.

__cgb_safe_set_bcpd

Write one byte to the background/window palette data register BCPD/BGPD at $FF69 on CGB. __cgb_safe_set_bgpd is another name for the same register. The associated index register selects the byte position; a full RGB15 color requires a low byte and a high byte.

This program teaches how palette contents affect the same tile pattern differently in BG and OBJ. Each triangle is 8 × 8 pixels; its top row has one pixel on the left, expanding to a full bottom row. At x = 16, 32, 48, 64, 80, 96, 112, the BG row at y = 24 is red, blue, green, red, blue, green, red. The OBJ row at y = 64 is blue, green, red, blue, green, red, blue. The columns use single-color, component, indexed-range, full-palette, absolute-range, canonical-register and alias-register writes, respectively.

At y = 88, the seven 8 × 8 cells show: red triangle (x = 16), blue one-pixel square outline from bank 1 (32), horizontally reflected green triangle (48), vertically reflected red triangle (64), blue triangle reflected both ways (80), green triangle over a red sprite with red visible through color-zero pixels (96), and a full red sprite in front of the BG triangle as a priority control (112). Coordinates are screen pixels measured from the upper-left corner. Blank space and black text remain on white.

FAILED CHECKS 000 confirms RGB masking, truth-valued flags (including input 2), palette index wrapping, writes crossing a palette boundary, clamping at slot 31, null/zero-length no-ops and palette readback while LCD is off. At x = 16, 32, 48, 64, 80, 96, 112, the BG row at y = 24 is red, blue, green, red, blue, green, red. The OBJ row at y = 64 is blue, green, red, blue, green, red, blue. The columns use single-color, component, indexed-range, full-palette, absolute-range, canonical-register and alias-register writes, respectively.

At y = 88, the seven 8 × 8 cells show: red triangle (x = 16), blue one-pixel square outline from bank 1 (32), horizontally reflected green triangle (48), vertically reflected red triangle (64), blue triangle reflected both ways (80), green triangle over a red sprite with red visible through color-zero pixels (96), and a full red sprite in front of the BG triangle as a priority control (112). Coordinates are screen pixels measured from the upper-left corner. Blank space and black text remain on white.

FAILED CHECKS 000 confirms RGB masking, truth-valued flags (including input 2), palette index wrapping, writes crossing a palette boundary, clamping at slot 31, null/zero-length no-ops and palette readback while LCD is off. Four target/hardware combinations are tested: dual-mode ROM on DMG and CGB, DMG-only on DMG, and CGB-only on CGB. On DMG, CGB MODE is 0, all triangles are black, the bank/flip attributes have no display effect, and the last two bottom cells are black squares. CGB MODE is 1 in CGB tests. Real hardware and LCD-on palette contention are not covered.

__cgb_is_cgb

Return the compiler's CGB-mode Boolean. An explicit --cgb=dmg target folds to 0 and --cgb=cgb_only to 1; a dual-mode --cgb=cgb target uses the runtime detector. This reports the selected execution mode, not every physical model feature.

Complete example program · Recorded checks

__cgb_safe_set_vbk

Select the CPU's VRAM bank through VBK ($FF4F) on CGB. Only value & 1 is written: even values select bank 0, odd values bank 1. This affects CPU accesses in $8000–$9FFF, not the tile-data bank selected by a map attribute. It does not restore the previous bank.

CGB_RGB15

Pack RGB components into a u16: red occupies bits 0–4, green 5–9 and blue 10–14; bit 15 is zero. Each component is masked with 31, so oversized values wrap instead of being clamped. This computes a value without writing hardware, on DMG as well as CGB.

KQ_CGB_ATTR_PRIORITY_IF

Build the BG-over-OBJ priority flag: 0x80 when enabled is nonzero, otherwise 0. With CGB master priority enabled (LCDC bit 0), nonzero BG color indices cover sprites. BG color index zero still lets sprites through; the flag does not make a tile opaque.

Build log

Complete example program · Recorded checks

__cgb_safe_set_hdma1

Write HDMA1 ($FF51), the high byte of the CGB DMA source address. This configures the source; it neither copies data nor starts a transfer. Set HDMA2 for the low byte.

The complete program compares three transfer paths using a 16-byte-aligned buffer at $C600. GDMA copies 32 bytes to $8010: at screen (16,24) a red 8 × 8 triangle grows from one leftmost pixel to a full bottom row, followed at (24,24) by a red one-pixel square outline. HBlank DMA copies 32 bytes to $8030: at (16,48) a blue 4 × 8 left half-tile is followed at (24,48) by a blue 8 × 8 square. These are actual transferred tile patterns, not numerical placeholders.

The CANCELED row contains a green 4 × 8 half-tile at (16,72) and a green 8 × 8 square at (24,72), initially written by the CPU. A two-block HBlank transfer targeting these tiles is armed and canceled in the same VBlank, before its first block; $FF55 must read $81, and both patterns must remain unchanged after a frame. On DMG, the first two rows stay blank and only these two sentinels appear in black. Text remains black on white. FAILED CHECKS 000 also checks copied bytes and completion status. The test covers dual-mode DMG/CGB and CGB-only execution in KOKURA; partial cancellation, double speed and real hardware are not tested.

Build log

Build log

Build log

Complete example program · Recorded checks

__cgb_safe_set_hdma2

Write HDMA2 ($FF52), the low byte of the DMA source address. Hardware ignores bits 0–3, aligning the source downward to 16 bytes. For example, HDMA1=$C6, HDMA2=$0F selects $C600. It does not start DMA.

The complete program compares three transfer paths using a 16-byte-aligned buffer at $C600. GDMA copies 32 bytes to $8010: at screen (16,24) a red 8 × 8 triangle grows from one leftmost pixel to a full bottom row, followed at (24,24) by a red one-pixel square outline. HBlank DMA copies 32 bytes to $8030: at (16,48) a blue 4 × 8 left half-tile is followed at (24,48) by a blue 8 × 8 square. These are actual transferred tile patterns, not numerical placeholders.

The test covers dual-mode DMG/CGB and CGB-only execution in KOKURA; partial cancellation, double speed and real hardware are not tested.

Complete example program · Recorded checks

__cgb_safe_set_hdma3

Write HDMA3 ($FF53), the high part of the DMA destination within VRAM. Only bits 0–4 are used; the destination is always in $8000–$9FFF. Pair it with HDMA4. This does not select the VRAM bank or start DMA.

The complete program compares three transfer paths using a 16-byte-aligned buffer at $C600. __cgb_safe_set_hdma4

Write HDMA4 ($FF54), the low byte of the VRAM destination address. Bits 0–3 are ignored, so the destination is aligned downward to 16 bytes. HDMA3=$E0, HDMA4=$1F selects $8010. This sets an address, not a transfer length.

The complete program compares three transfer paths using a 16-byte-aligned buffer at $C600. GDMA copies 32 bytes to $8010: at screen (16,24) a red 8 × 8 triangle grows from one leftmost pixel to a full bottom row, followed at (24,24) by a red one-pixel square outline. Complete example program · Recorded checks

__cgb_safe_set_hdma5

Write HDMA5 ($FF55) to start DMA, or cancel an active HBlank transfer. For a new transfer, bits 0–6 encode block count minus one and bit 7 chooses GDMA (0) or HBlank DMA (1). During an active HBlank transfer, writing bit 7 clear cancels the remaining work; it does not start a new GDMA transfer.

The complete program compares three transfer paths using a 16-byte-aligned buffer at $C600. GDMA copies 32 bytes to $8010: at screen (16,24) a red 8 × 8 triangle grows from one leftmost pixel to a full bottom row, followed at (24,24) by a red one-pixel square outline. HBlank DMA copies 32 bytes to $8030: at (16,48) a blue 4 × 8 left half-tile is followed at (24,48) by a blue 8 × 8 square. These are actual transferred tile patterns, not numerical placeholders.

The CANCELED row contains a green 4 × 8 half-tile at (16,72) and a green 8 × 8 square at (24,72), initially written by the CPU. A two-block HBlank transfer targeting these tiles is armed and canceled in the same VBlank, before its first block; $FF55 must read $81, and both patterns must remain unchanged after a frame. On DMG, the first two rows stay blank and only these two sentinels appear in black. Text remains black on white. FAILED CHECKS 000 also checks copied bytes and completion status. The test covers dual-mode DMG/CGB and CGB-only execution in KOKURA; partial cancellation, double speed and real hardware are not tested.

Build log

Build log

Build log

Complete example program · Recorded checks

__cgb_safe_set_svbk

Write the raw WRAM selector SVBK ($FF70) on CGB. Hardware uses bits 0–2; raw values 0 and 1 both map bank 1 at $D000–$DFFF. For example, 8 also maps bank 1. Unlike __svbk_set, this guarded setter permits a dual-mode target and does not return the previous bank.

The complete CGB-only program stores 1 at $D100 in WRAM bank 2 and 2 at the same address in bank 3, then reads both back and restores bank 1. The values choose an 8 × 8 red square at (16,24) and a blue one-pixel 8 × 8 square outline at (16,48). FAILED CHECKS 000 checks raw-selector normalization, bank separation and the previous-bank return for constant, variable, arithmetic and function-result arguments. Six separate invalid-build fixtures verify KQ2103 for DMG/dual targets and KQ2102 for constant banks 0 and 8. Runtime verification uses KOKURA; real hardware and interrupt-driven concurrent bank changes are not tested.

Build log

Complete example program · Recorded checks

__svbk_get

Read the effective WRAM bank mapped at $D000–$DFFF. The compiler reads SVBK bits 0–2 and converts raw selector 0 to bank 1. This returns a bank number, not RAM capacity, an address or a pointer.

The complete CGB-only program stores 1 at $D100 in WRAM bank 2 and 2 at the same address in bank 3, then reads both back and restores bank 1. The values choose an 8 × 8 red square at (16,24) and a blue one-pixel 8 × 8 square outline at (16,48). FAILED CHECKS 000 checks raw-selector normalization, bank separation and the previous-bank return for constant, variable, arithmetic and function-result arguments. Six separate invalid-build fixtures verify KQ2103 for DMG/dual targets and KQ2102 for constant banks 0 and 8. Runtime verification uses KOKURA; real hardware and interrupt-driven concurrent bank changes are not tested.

Build log

KQ2102 / KQ2103

Complete example program · Recorded checks

__svbk_set

Switch the WRAM bank mapped at $D000–$DFFF and return the effective bank selected before the switch. Save this return value if you need to restore the caller's bank. The memory contents are not copied or cleared, and the function does not restore the bank automatically.

The complete CGB-only program stores 1 at $D100 in WRAM bank 2 and 2 at the same address in bank 3, then reads both back and restores bank 1. The values choose an 8 × 8 red square at (16,24) and a blue one-pixel 8 × 8 square outline at (16,48). FAILED CHECKS 000 checks raw-selector normalization, bank separation and the previous-bank return for constant, variable, arithmetic and function-result arguments. Six separate invalid-build fixtures verify KQ2103 for DMG/dual targets and KQ2102 for constant banks 0 and 8. Runtime verification uses KOKURA; real hardware and interrupt-driven concurrent bank changes are not tested.

KQ2102 / KQ2103

Complete example program · Recorded checks

asset_set_table

Register the bank, address and entry count of the single active descriptor table. The function stores these three values; it does not copy the table, load any asset or allocate memory.

This example registers four descriptors in bank 3 whose nonempty payloads are in bank 2. It also registers an empty table and verifies that lookup fails without reading address zero.

Expected image: a red row starts at pixel (16,32) and spans 72×8 pixels. It repeats an 8×8 triangle, an 8×8 square outline with a one-pixel border, and a tile with its left four columns filled, three times. The triangle grows from one pixel at the top left to eight pixels on its bottom row.

On GB, a blue triangle and outline at (16,64) show the __farmemcpy result; a green pair at (16,96) shows __far_memcpy. Each pair occupies 16×8 pixels. CGB shows these colors; DMG shows the same shapes in black on white.

Expected status: FAILED CHECKS 000 and FIRST FAILURE 00. The verification runs 300 frames and compares text, shape masks and colors against these expectations. This is emulator evidence, not a physical-hardware test.

Copy one AssetDesc from the registered table’s ROM bank into caller-provided storage. This retrieves the resource’s metadata, not its payload.

Complete example program · Recorded checks

asset_load_raw

Look up a resource and copy min(desc.len, max_len) bytes from its ROM bank to writable memory. The type tag is ignored: tile, map and song data are copied as bytes without decoding or decompression.

A seven-byte limit copies only the first seven bytes of a 144-byte resource and leaves the next byte at 0x55, yet returns success. The program also checks a full copy, a zero limit, an empty resource and raw copying of a MAP-tagged resource.

asset_load_tiles

Complete example program · Recorded checks

__farmemcpy

Temporarily select a ROM bank, copy len bytes from src to dst in increasing address order, then restore the compiler’s saved ROM bank. __farmemcpy and __far_memcpy use the same implementation on this platform.

Copy two patterns, 32 bytes, from payload bank 2 while bank 1 is initially selected. The program compares every copied byte, checks that bank 1 is restored and verifies that a zero-length copy leaves a 0x55 sentinel unchanged.

asset_load_tiles

Look up a RAW or TILES resource, select its ROM bank and upload all bytes with __vram_write in chunks of at most 128 bytes. Restore the bank saved by bank_get_current() afterwards. Chunking does not wait for another frame.

The tile-upload example uses nine original 8×8 patterns, 144 bytes in total. It verifies that MAP and invalid IDs are rejected. On FC, 144 bytes exercise both the 128-byte chunk and the remaining 16 bytes.

Build log

Complete example program · Recorded checks

bank_switch

Select the ROM bank and record it in both the bank library and the compiler's bank state. Far reads and calls use the compiler state to restore the previous mapping.

The initial bank_switch(2) records and selects bank 2. Later, direct switching selects bank 1 and reads A1 from home1 while bank_get_current still reports 2; switching back makes home2 read B2 again.

The complete program teaches how a bank number and near address identify data, how callbacks cross banks, and how to distinguish the library's record from the actual mapping. All displayed numbers are hexadecimal. BANK IN and BANK OUT must show 02, BYTE HEX D3, WORD HEX 5C7A, and both FAILED CHECKS and FIRST FAILURE 00. CGB and FC display blue text; DMG displays black text.

CALL COUNT is 03: far_call, __farcall and __farcall_ptr each invoke the callback once.

__bankswitch

Write the low bank byte to cartridge address $2000 and update the compiler's bank state. The selection remains in effect after this call; bank_get_current is not updated.

__bankof

Obtain the logical ROM-bank number assigned to a named data object or function. This resolves placement information; it does not inspect the currently mapped bank or dereference a pointer.

The sample checks that both payload and callback are placed in bank 3. This proves placement metadata; separate reads and callback checks prove the runtime bank behavior.

farptr_make

Store a bank byte and a CPU-visible pointer in the caller's BankPtr. This constructs a descriptor; it does not switch banks or read the pointed-to bytes.

The descriptor is set to bank 3 and payload+1. Its readers then obtain 7A, 5C7A and the three bytes 7A 5C 96. The null-output call must leave all state unchanged.

farptr_read

Copy len bytes from a BankPtr into writable RAM. The descriptor is passed by value, so the caller's pointer still identifies the start of the source.

This excerpt copies 3 bytes through farptr_read and checks the buffer against 7A 5C 96. The source descriptor or pointer is retained by the caller; the full program separately checks a zero-length copy and bank restoration.

The complete program teaches how a bank number and near address identify data, how callbacks cross banks, and how to distinguish the library's record from the actual mapping. All displayed numbers are hexadecimal. BANK IN and BANK OUT must show 02, BYTE HEX D3, WORD HEX 5C7A, and both FAILED CHECKS and FIRST FAILURE 00. CGB and FC display blue text; DMG displays black text.

CALL COUNT is 03: far_call, __farcall and __farcall_ptr each invoke the callback once.

far_call

Forward a bank number and callback pointer to __farcall_ptr. This library function invokes a zero-argument callback and returns only after the caller's bank has been restored.

At this point in the complete program, far_call raises callback_count to 1. The callback reads D3 from bank 3. A later near read of B2 from home2 verifies that execution returned with bank 2 mapped.

__farcall

Call a named, zero-argument function using the supplied bank number. When a bank change is needed, a helper in fixed ROM restores the caller's bank after the function returns.

At this point in the complete program, __farcall raises callback_count to 2. The callback reads D3 from bank 3. A later near read of B2 from home2 verifies that execution returned with bank 2 mapped.

__farcall_ptr

Invoke a zero-argument callback at a runtime address in the supplied bank. The fixed-ROM helper saves each caller's bank on the CPU stack and restores it on return. The intrinsic returns no value.

At this point in the complete program, __farcall_ptr raises callback_count to 3. The callback reads D3 from bank 3. A later near read of B2 from home2 verifies that execution returned with bank 2 mapped.

bank_switch

Record the requested bank in the library, then select it through the compiler's mapper-specific PRG-bank helper.

The initial bank_switch(2) records and selects bank 2. Later, direct switching selects bank 1 and reads A1 from home1 while bank_get_current still reports 2; switching back makes home2 read B2 again.

__bankof

Obtain the logical ROM-bank number assigned to a named data object or function. This resolves placement information; it does not inspect the currently mapped bank or dereference a pointer.

The sample checks that both payload and callback are placed in bank 3. This proves placement metadata; separate reads and callback checks prove the runtime bank behavior.

farptr_make

Store a bank byte and a CPU-visible pointer in the caller's BankPtr. This constructs a descriptor; it does not switch banks or read the pointed-to bytes.

The descriptor is set to bank 3 and payload+1. Its readers then obtain 7A, 5C7A and the three bytes 7A 5C 96. The null-output call must leave all state unchanged.

Complete example program · Recorded checks

sprite_init

Reset the sprite allocator and hide every slot in shadow OAM. Set the software sprite height to 8 pixels; configuring the hardware sprite size and transferring OAM are separate steps.

Initialization yields zero allocated slots. Eight allocations return IDs 0..7. Hiding slot 6 keeps the count at 8; freeing slot 7 twice leaves 7. Placing the pair in slots 10 and 11 raises the count to 9.

This program teaches sprite allocation, positioning, flips, composite objects, overlap, animation and the scanline limit. The two digits at the upper right must be 00: every in-program check passed. Each arrow and square occupies an 8×8 tile.

NORMAL shows a right-pointing arrow at (88,16). FLIP X points left at (88,32); FLIP Y vertically mirrors the arrow at (88,48). META places two arrows at (88,64) and (96,64). OVERLAP puts the arrow at (88,80) in front of the square at (92,80), with the square visible through transparent arrow pixels. ANIMATION shows the square at (88,96). The areas at (120,16) and (120,32) remain empty after hiding and freeing their slots.

CGB and FC arrows use a green shaft, red outline and blue tip details; the square is green. Transparent pixels reveal the white GB background or black FC background. DMG uses light gray, dark gray and black instead of red, green and blue.

The row below SCAN LIMIT starts at (8,128). The program submits 11 GB sprites or 9 FC sprites on that line, deliberately one too many. Expect 10 visible arrows on GB and 8 on FC; the final submitted arrow is absent. The software peak and warning checks must still report 11/9 and 1.

sprite_count_used

Return the number of allocated slots, including hidden sprites and slots activated by metasprite_draw. This is an allocation count, not the number of sprites visible on screen.

Initialization yields zero allocated slots. Eight allocations return IDs 0..7. Hiding slot 6 keeps the count at 8; freeing slot 7 twice leaves 7. Placing the pair in slots 10 and 11 raises the count to 9.

This program teaches sprite allocation, positioning, flips, composite objects, overlap, animation and the scanline limit. The two digits at the upper right must be 00: every in-program check passed. Each arrow and square occupies an 8×8 tile.

NORMAL shows a right-pointing arrow at (88,16). FLIP X points left at (88,32); FLIP Y vertically mirrors the arrow at (88,48). META places two arrows at (88,64) and (96,64). OVERLAP puts the arrow at (88,80) in front of the square at (92,80), with the square visible through transparent arrow pixels. ANIMATION shows the square at (88,96). The areas at (120,16) and (120,32) remain empty after hiding and freeing their slots.

CGB and FC arrows use a green shaft, red outline and blue tip details; the square is green. Transparent pixels reveal the white GB background or black FC background. DMG uses light gray, dark gray and black instead of red, green and blue.

The row below SCAN LIMIT starts at (8,128). The program submits 11 GB sprites or 9 FC sprites on that line, deliberately one too many. Expect 10 visible arrows on GB and 8 on FC; the final submitted arrow is absent. The software peak and warning checks must still report 11/9 and 1.

Build log

Build log

metasprite_draw

Place a counted array of sprite parts in consecutive slots. Each part supplies a signed offset, tile and attributes. The routine activates new slots, overwrites existing active slots and stops at the slot limit.

Initialization yields zero allocated slots. Eight allocations return IDs 0..7. Hiding slot 6 keeps the count at 8; freeing slot 7 twice leaves 7. Placing the pair in slots 10 and 11 raises the count to 9.

anim_update

Advance a SpriteAnim timer and return the selected tile number. After ticks_per_frame calls, move to the next consecutive tile; wrap to the first frame after frame_count frames. The caller applies the result with sprite_set_tile.

With first_tile=128, two frames and two calls per frame, the first update returns arrow tile 128 and the second returns square tile 129. The square shown beside ANIMATION proves that the returned tile was applied to OAM.

sprite_init

Reset the sprite allocator and hide every slot in shadow OAM. Set the software sprite height to 8 pixels; configuring the hardware sprite size and transferring OAM are separate steps.
